The continuation state is a physical cursor — the byte offset of the first unprocessed line in the verified snapshot copy — persisted atomically (temp-file + rename) in the replay-owned traversal sidecar at cycle end and on every deadline interruption; the cursor advances past every fully-processed line whether that line was a tombstone absorbed into the batch winner map (its delete intent durable there until the batch's veto scan applies it, LWW-loses it, no-ops on an absent row, or vetoes it as a canonical loss — R48 F1), a `_deleted: true` line resolved as the R48 F2 invalid-id diagnostic, or a non-tombstone record skipped without database work, so scanning progress through arbitrarily long non-tombstone stretches is durable and a deadline may expire at ANY line boundary — the next cycle, or a restarted daemon, resumes at exactly that byte with zero skipped records and zero re-scan. A byte offset is safe here for exactly the reason R33 rejected it elsewhere: it targets the replay-owned verified snapshot copy — a file no other writer opens, digest-verified at acquisition and re-verified on restart resume — never the replaceable live file, so no replacement detection or rebase exists to get wrong (R43 F1). Application is canonical-winner gated (R48 F1): a tombstone is applied only when it is its id's winner across the whole verified snapshot under the same `newer_record` rule the import path's canonicalization delegates to for same-id live/tombstone collisions (`_merge_memory_records`, `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:200-203` -> `newer_record`, `src/gobby/sync/tombstones.py:35-45`: greater timestamp wins, deletion preferred on exact ties) — because `apply_tombstone` LWW-compares only against the current database row, direct per-line application would let a duplicate-bearing foreign generation delete a local row from an older tombstone even when a strictly newer live record for the same id sits elsewhere in the same snapshot, an outcome the import path structurally forecloses by canonicalizing the parsed record set before applying anything. The gate keeps the pass sort-free and bounded: traversal is per-batch — the discovery cursor scans forward in physical line order absorbing parsed tombstones into a per-id batch winner map (maximum `deleted_at` per id, same-id repeats collapse) whose capacity is `TOMBSTONE_REPLAY_MAX_RECORDS_PER_CYCLE`, a full batch's applies being exactly one cycle's application budget; a veto scan — one additional full linear pass over the verified snapshot, chunked, deadline-honored at every chunk boundary, classification and ceiling semantics identical to the discovery scan, with its own byte cursor — then records for exactly the batch's ids the maximum admitted live-record timestamp, where admission is the import path's own live-record contract, shared as one pure helper between `_import_memories_from_lines` and the veto scan and applied in import's order (R49 F1): record validation (non-empty string `content`, list-or-convertible `tags`), content sanitization, ephemeral-implementation-note filtering on the sanitized record, then timestamp normalization — a non-empty malformed `created_at` or `updated_at` makes the line inadmissible exactly as import skips it, a missing `updated_at` inherits `created_at` (and conversely), and the recorded value is the post-normalization `updated_at`, the exact field `record_timestamp` feeds `newer_record` during import canonicalization; a live record missing both timestamps is stamped with the shared helper's own `utc_now()` at veto admission — the identical stamping import performs at its admission — so the recorded maximum is always a concrete timestamp, persisted as such in batch state, and the ordinary `newer_record` ordering resolves it with no marker special case: a past-dated tombstone loses to the stamp and is vetoed, while a future-dated tombstone wins and applies its committed delete, exactly as import canonicalization resolves the same snapshot — tombstone admission accepts any parseable `deleted_at` and `newer_record` prefers any strictly greater timestamp, future-dated included, so an unconditional veto would silently strand a delete intent import honors; both paths are clock-relative at their own processing time, and the retired marker's clock-free determinism claim is withdrawn as unsound (R50 F1); a line that fails classification or any step of the admission chain vetoes nothing — import excludes exactly those records from canonicalization, so they defend no row there either, and the alignment holds in both directions; and each batch id whose tombstone timestamp is greater than or equal to its observed live maximum applies through the same LWW-guarded per-tombstone transaction, while a vetoed id resolves as a canonical-loss no-op with zero database statements — the snapshot's own newer live record owns that id's fate through the import path, never the replay. Batch state — the winner map, the per-id admitted live maxima in their post-normalization form (every entry a concrete timestamp, admission-time `utc_now()` stamps included — R50 F1), and both scan cursors — persists atomically in the traversal sidecar under the same temp-file-plus-rename discipline, bounded by the same named record constant, so a deadline may expire at any chunk or line boundary of either scan and resume with zero re-discovery; the veto costs at most one additional full snapshot scan per batch — at most one per `TOMBSTONE_REPLAY_MAX_RECORDS_PER_CYCLE` tombstones per pass — every byte of it deadline-covered cycle work. The veto runs uniformly in every generation, trusted and untrusted alike: on a trusted generation it is structurally a no-op — the canonicalizing exporter already resolves same-id collisions through `newer_record` before writing, so an exporter-written generation cannot carry a same-id tombstone/live pair — and the trusted over-cap skip-without-parse shortcut is unaffected for exactly that reason. When the cursor reaches snapshot end-of-input the pass is complete: the replay deletes the snapshot copy and clears the traversal sidecar — the trust sidecar is untouched, structurally: it has a different owner and the replay never writes it (R43 F2), and trust names a property of the live generation, not of any traversal (R40 F1) — and the next cycle acquires the then-current sync file as a fresh verified snapshot copy — the wrap-around that guarantees every tombstone, including one introduced at any position by any mid-pass mutation of the live file, atomic replacement and in-place rewrite alike, is visited within one full pass of appearing; snapshot staleness is bounded by one pass. A missing or unparseable traversal sidecar, a cursor beyond snapshot EOF, or a vanished, truncated, or digest-mismatched snapshot copy degrades to a fresh pass — drop the copy and the traversal sidecar and re-acquire from the live sync file; no traversal degradation touches the trust sidecar, whose only writer is the merge exporter (R43 F2), and a trust sidecar that is itself missing or unparseable reverts the generation to untrusted in the safe direction, which costs at most redundant over-cap parses until the next export stamp and can never produce a wrongly trusted skip — and the replay is idempotent through the same LWW-guarded per-tombstone transactions, so the cursor and snapshot are fairness devices, never correctness dependencies. Progress guarantee (R35 F1): no unit of mandatory work between two deadline checks is unbounded. The scan reads the snapshot in fixed-size chunks (`TOMBSTONE_REPLAY_SCAN_CHUNK_BYTES`, named module constant, default 1 MiB) and checks the deadline at every chunk boundary as well as every line boundary — the R34 rule that a full line is always processed before any deadline check is retired, because a single line has no size bound anywhere in the pipeline: sync import validation accepts any non-empty content string (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:528-531`), storage creation rejects only empty content (`src/gobby/storage/memories_crud.py:40-43`), sanitization substitutes without truncating (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:598-622`), and export serializes each record as exactly one JSONL line (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:755-764`). When the deadline expires mid-line the cycle ends with the durable cursor still at that line's start — the traversal sidecar stays line-granular and crash-safe — while the replay object carries the intra-line scan position in memory across cycles within the daemon lifetime, so the next cycle resumes scanning where the last one stopped; a daemon restart forfeits only that intra-line position and re-scans from the line-start cursor — after re-verifying the retained snapshot copy against its recorded digest (chunked, deadline-bounded; a mismatch degrades to a fresh acquisition, R43 F1) — a one-time per-restart rework of at most one line plus one bounded re-verification, never a per-cycle redo, so convergence holds absent a crash loop. Parsing is bounded by classification, and the classification is made sound by enforcing the bounded tombstone shape at both sync-file boundaries (R36 F1): tombstone records are fixed-shape five-field markers (`id`, `project_id`, `_deleted`, `deleted_at`, `updated_at`) built by `load_tombstones` (`src/gobby/sync/tombstones.py:100-108`), so DB-sourced tombstones are bounded by construction — but the file boundaries today admit unbounded impostors: import accepts any `_deleted: true` record whose `id` is a string and whose timestamp parses, with no restriction on extra fields or serialized size (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:438-447`), while export preserves existing file-borne tombstone dictionaries verbatim (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:678-686`), carries them through dedup as contentless records (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:567-571`), and re-serializes every key (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:121-127`), so an accepted over-cap tombstone would be re-emitted forever and its delete intent would hide behind the size shortcut. Both boundaries are therefore tightened against the same named constant: memory import normalizes a tombstone's `project_id` at admission — canonical UUID string, or null when the value is not a valid UUID or is oversized (R50 F2) — and fails visibly (`MemoryImportError`, raised before any transaction begins) when the normalized record's serialized line still exceeds `TOMBSTONE_REPLAY_MAX_TOMBSTONE_LINE_BYTES` — never a silent skip, because silently dropping a delete intent enables resurrect-by-merge — and export canonicalizes every tombstone it emits to exactly the five durable fields, rebuilding file-borne tombstone dicts into the `load_tombstones` shape instead of preserving them verbatim, and resolving file-borne tombstone defects by provability (R48 F2, revised by R51 F1): a `_deleted: true` line whose `id` fails UUID validation is vacuous by column type — it can never name a `memories` row — so the exporter omits it from the canonical output as a named, counted vacuous-tombstone diagnostic and proceeds to publish and trust-stamp, at any line size, losing no delete intent because there is none to lose — with omission-aware shrink accounting (R52 F1): the exporter's shrink guard compares the canonical count against a baseline that excludes exactly the counted vacuous-tombstone omissions, because the raw baseline admits any parsed line with a truthy id (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:678-686`) and the guard would otherwise raise `MemoryExportError` on the very omission this rule requires (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:748-753`, `force` defaulting to false at `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:379`), re-wedging the cursor the omission exists to free; every other canonical-count shrink — duplicate-id or normalized/fuzzy dedup collapse, record loss — still fails without `force` exactly as today; while a valid-UUID-id tombstone whose remaining durable fields are missing or unparseable names a row whose real delete intent cannot be soundly applied or discarded, and only that residue fails the export visibly (`MemoryExportError` naming the line and id, raised before the atomic write so the prior file survives intact, no trust stamp). The tombstone id boundary is UUID-typed end to end: `memories.id` is declared `UUID PRIMARY KEY` in the baseline schema, and `apply_tombstone` binds the tombstone's id string directly into that column's equality predicate, so a non-UUID id can never name a local row — its delete intent is vacuous by column type — and binding it would raise PostgreSQL invalid-uuid syntax instead of applying, turning any promised over-cap-by-id application into a crash. All three boundaries therefore validate tombstone `id` as a parseable UUID: import rejects an invalid id visibly in the parse phase (`MemoryImportError` naming the line and offending id, raised before any transaction begins — the same visibility contract as the over-cap line check); the merge exporter's canonicalization omits it from the canonical output as a named, counted vacuous-tombstone diagnostic — mirroring the replay's own resolution — and publishes and trust-stamps the remaining generation (R51 F1: the retired fail-visibly export exit wedged the over-ceiling variant of exactly this input); and the replay resolves a `_deleted: true` line whose id fails UUID validation as a named, counted invalid-tombstone diagnostic that advances the cursor without ever binding a database statement — id validation precedes durable-field validation, since an invalid id names nothing and skipping it honors everything there is to honor, while a valid-id tombstone whose remaining durable fields are missing or unparseable keeps its fail-visibly-without-cursor-advance semantics because it names a row whose real delete intent cannot be safely resolved; the unattended pass therefore stays starvation-free with the generation still untrusted. Canonicalizing the field set alone does not bound the line, because the durable values themselves are unbounded for foreign records — import accepts any string `id` with a parseable timestamp and never validates `project_id` (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:442-447`), serialization preserves field values verbatim (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:121-127`), and a git-arrived generation never crosses the import boundary at all: the merge exporter re-parses the live file directly — so an exactly-five-field tombstone whose `project_id` alone exceeds the cap would survive canonicalization over-cap — the `id` axis cannot carry the excess, because ids are UUID-validated at every tombstone boundary (R48 F2) and a valid id is bounded at canonical UUID width. The exporter therefore closes the value axis by canonicalization rather than rejection (R44 F1, revised by R50 F2): while rebuilding each canonical five-field dict (`id`, `project_id`, `_deleted`, `deleted_at`, `updated_at` — the `load_tombstones` shape) it normalizes `project_id` to a canonical UUID string, or to null when the value is not a valid UUID or is oversized — tombstone application is id-based, `apply_tombstone` never reads `project_id`, so an invalid or bloated scope value carries no delete semantics worth preserving — and the same normalization runs at the import admission boundary and at the replay parse that feeds the batch winner map, keeping all three boundaries shape-identical. Every canonical five-field line is then bounded by construction — UUID id, UUID-or-null `project_id`, boolean `_deleted`, two canonically serialized parseable timestamps — and the serialized-size check on the canonical output line (`MemoryExportError`, raised while building the canonical output — before the byte-identical comparison, before `atomic_write_text`, and before either trust-stamp exit, so the prior file survives intact and no trust identity is written) survives only as a defensive invariant no reachable tombstone trips: the retired fail-visibly design wedged exactly the input it meant to surface, because a tombstone whose `project_id` exceeded even the foreign-line ceiling could neither be parsed by replay nor exported past the cap, stranding its delete intent with no remediation (R50 F2). With both boundaries enforced on the field-set and value axes alike, every tombstone line in any accepted import or exporter-written sync file is bounded by construction, and a line longer than `TOMBSTONE_REPLAY_MAX_TOMBSTONE_LINE_BYTES` (named module constant, default 64 KiB, orders of magnitude above any real tombstone) cannot be a tombstone and is skipped without ever being buffered or JSON-parsed — its bytes are only scanned chunk-by-chunk for the terminating newline under the deadline. The skip-without-parse shortcut is additionally gated by generation trust (R37 F1), because the R36 boundary guarantees cover only files the enforcing importer accepted or the canonicalizing exporter wrote, while the file a replay pass pins can predate or bypass both boundaries — the pre-upgrade file already on disk at first startup, a foreign generation arriving mid-run through git (the sync file is git-native), or a hand edit — and today's code demonstrably produces such files (permissive import `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:438-447`, verbatim export preserve `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:678-686`) while `start_periodic_tasks` launches `memory_reconcile_loop` shortly after startup with no ordering against any export (`src/gobby/runner_lifecycle_periodic.py:220-227`, `src/gobby/runner_maintenance_recurring.py:148-188`; sync import and export remain separate explicit daemon actions). The replay therefore trusts only generations whose bounded canonical shape this daemon itself durably produced: the trust identity lives in a dedicated trust sidecar whose only writer is the merge exporter, physically separate from the replay-owned traversal sidecar (R43 F2): one shared sidecar under two uncoordinated whole-file writers would be a read-modify-write lost update — atomic temp-file replacement prevents torn files but provides no merge, so a replay traversal persist could erase a just-stamped trust identity (stranding the promised over-ceiling remediation) and an exporter stamp could regress a just-advanced cursor (breaking starvation-free continuation); single-writer ownership removes the race with no new locking, per-file atomic replacement then sufficing. The trust sidecar records the generation identity of the last sync file content this daemon durably proved canonical. Generation identity is defined content-bound (R41 F1): it is the SHA-256 digest of the file's complete byte content, and a stamped identity always names exact bytes the stamper held in memory — never a later re-read or stat of the live path — because the export lock is only an advisory `fcntl` flock on a separate sidecar lock file (`src/gobby/sync/jsonl_io.py:23-46`) that real writers bypass: worktree merge resolution lists `.gobby/memories.jsonl` as a trivial conflict and overwrites it with `git checkout --theirs` with no lock participation (`src/gobby/worktrees/merge/resolver.py:34-37`, `:60-85`), and plain git operations and hand edits likewise, so a foreign generation can replace the live file at any instant before, during, or after a stamp. The identity is written and proved solely by the merge exporter — the replay never writes it (R42 F1) — which under R36 already canonicalizes every tombstone it emits and which stamps the trust sidecar while still holding the export lock on BOTH of its exits (R40 F1) with the SAME in-memory value: the digest of its freshly built canonical output (today's `new_hash`, `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:765`) — on the replace exit these are the exact bytes just handed to `atomic_write_text`, and on the byte-identical fast path — today's exporter returns before `atomic_write_text` when that digest equals the digest of the bytes it read from the live file (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:767-776`), still inside the export lock acquired at `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:781-782` — it is provably the digest of the exact bytes just verified, an equal-or-stronger canonicality proof, because hash equality proves the read bytes are exactly what the canonicalizing exporter would have written; so routine exports never strand the live file untrusted regardless of which exit they take, and an unlocked foreign replacement racing either exit can at worst leave the FOREIGN generation untrusted (its content hashes differently, costing at most redundant over-cap parses until the next export stamp) — it can never cause unverified bytes to be trusted, because the stamp never derives its value from the live path after verification. On the replay side, trust is decided per pass by a content proof over the verified snapshot copy, never over the live path — and under R43 F1 that proof is in hand before traversal begins: snapshot acquisition itself computed the generation's complete digest and proved it stable, so the per-pass verdict (verified digest equals the trust sidecar's recorded identity: trusted; anything else: untrusted) is decided exactly once at verification time and cached as pass state, with zero additional hashing I/O during traversal. The R41 lazy proof — a running digest accumulated while scanning, completed by chunked read-ahead at the first over-cap encounter — is retired as unnecessary mechanism: copy-and-verify already reads every snapshot byte, so there is no hashing to defer and no cost to avoid on passes without over-cap lines; and an in-place foreign mutation now surfaces earlier and stronger — as an acquisition-time verification mismatch that discards the copy before any byte is traversed, in every generation and for every line size — rather than as a mere untrusted verdict (R43 F1). Only a pinned generation whose scanned content digest equals that recorded identity is trusted; its over-cap lines are provably non-tombstones under the R36 canonical-tombstone invariant and skip freely. In an untrusted generation over-cap lines are handled in place, without ever mutating the sync file (R42 F1). The earlier replay-owned canonicalization rewrite (R38) was retired because its publication step was an incurable check-then-act race: the export lock is an advisory flock real writers bypass, `atomic_write_text` ends in an unconditional `os.replace` (`src/gobby/sync/jsonl_io.py:49-68`), and POSIX offers no compare-and-swap on a path — so a foreign generation arriving between the publication re-verify and the replace would have been silently overwritten by a transform of the older pinned snapshot, destroying newly arrived records and delete intents; and routing canonicalization through the merge exporter had already been rejected (R38: `export_to_files` delegates wholesale to an `asyncio.to_thread` worker, `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:399-405`, that blocks indefinitely acquiring `LOCK_EX`, re-parses every file line and pages the entire database, `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:669-706`, and collapses duplicate ids and near-duplicate content, `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:547-596`, so a duplicate-bearing foreign generation legitimately shrinks into the shrink guard's `MemoryExportError`, `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:748-753`). Neither mechanism is needed: the replay simply parses each over-cap line within the foreign-line ceiling (bounded accumulation per R39 below) and, when the parse classifies it a tombstone, applies its committed delete through the identical application path as an under-cap tombstone line — a non-tombstone or malformed line is passed over exactly like its under-cap counterpart. The replay therefore performs zero writes to the sync file path: no temp file beside it, no export-lock acquisition, no `os.replace`, no replay-written trust stamp — read-only with respect to the sync file, which eliminates the entire observation-to-publication race class rather than narrowing its window. The file itself becomes canonical the next time any routine or explicit export replaces or verifies it and stamps trust through the two-exit stamp; until then each periodic pass re-parses the untrusted generation's over-cap lines within the ceiling — bounded per-pass work under the existing chunk, cap, and deadline constants, and harmless to repeat because tombstone application is idempotent under the R31/R32 arm state machine. Classification of over-cap lines is itself bounded (R39 F1): tombstone-ness is a property of the decoded record, not the raw bytes — `is_tombstone` examines the parsed dictionary (`src/gobby/sync/tombstones.py:17-19`) and both existing line consumers materialize complete lines and `json.loads` them before classifying (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py:440`, `src/gobby/sync/memories.py:680`) — and no sound sub-line screen exists, because a `_deleted` key can be escape-encoded (`\u005f`) or overridden by JSON duplicate-key last-wins semantics, so the only exact alternative is a resumable streaming JSON classifier carrying intra-line lexer state across cycles, deliberately rejected as unjustified mechanism for a pathological tail. Instead the classification parse is capped by a second named module constant, `TOMBSTONE_REPLAY_MAX_FOREIGN_LINE_BYTES` (default 8 MiB — 128x the tombstone line cap and far above any legitimate record this codebase has produced): an under-cap line is buffered and parsed exactly as the replay parses it; an over-cap line within the ceiling is accumulated across scan chunks with the deadline still honored at every chunk boundary while the buffer grows (intra-line position carries in memory, the durable cursor stays line-granular, and a restart reworks at most one line of at most ceiling bytes), then classified with one whole-line parse bounded by the ceiling — a parsed tombstone enters the current batch's per-id winner map from that parse (application deferred to the batch's canonical-winner gate — R48 F1 — with a UUID-invalid id resolving instead as the R48 F2 named diagnostic), and everything else, malformed JSON included, is passed over untouched. A line exceeding the ceiling in an untrusted generation fails the cycle visibly with no cursor advance and no silent skip: it cannot be proven a non-tombstone within bounded work, and skipping it would silently strand a possible delete intent (the resurrect-by-merge hazard). The named remediation is one operator action: an explicit memory export emits every tombstone canonically under-cap and stamps the digest of the exact bytes it published or verified — never a later re-read of the live path (R41 F1) — trusted through the two-exit R40 `export_locked` stamp: the digest of the bytes handed to `atomic_write_text` after an atomic replacement, or the digest of the verified bytes when the untrusted generation is already byte-identical to the exporter's canonical output and the export returns on the unchanged-hash fast path — so remediation succeeds even for a generation already in canonical exporter form (for example a git-arrived copy of another machine's export containing a legitimately over-ceiling non-tombstone record), after which the replay skips the oversized line unparsed under the trusted invariant; and because the trust sidecar is untouched by pass completion and traversal resets (single-writer ownership, R43 F2), later complete periodic passes over that generation neither fail visibly nor re-parse it. Self-healing therefore holds for every untrusted generation whose lines fit within the foreign-line ceiling — delete intents heal into the database immediately from the parse, and the file heals to canonical form at the next export — while beyond the ceiling it degrades to a visible failure whose named remediation terminates on every input (R50 F2, completed by R51 F1): the explicit export re-parses the live file with no line ceiling — one operator action bounded by file size, not a deadline-covered cycle — so an over-ceiling line that parses as a tombstone with a valid UUID id is normalized under-cap and re-emitted canonically, and an over-ceiling `_deleted: true` line whose id fails UUID validation is omitted as the named vacuous-tombstone diagnostic — the combined case bounded replay can never classify and the retired fail-visibly export exit could never clear, which would have starved every later valid tombstone behind a permanently wedged cursor (R51 F1) — so the post-replace generation carries no over-ceiling tombstone line, and the next replay pass parses the canonical lines and applies every committed delete, while an over-ceiling non-tombstone record survives the rewrite and is thereafter skipped unparsed under the trusted invariant. The sole residue export cannot mechanically resolve — a valid-UUID-id tombstone whose remaining durable fields are missing or unparseable, a real delete intent with unknowable ordering — fails the export visibly naming the line and id with the prior file intact, a bounded operator edit away from resolution, matching the replay's own fail-visibly-without-advance semantics for the same input; never an unbounded work unit, never a silent skip. Under-cap lines are buffered and parsed as before in every generation regardless of trust, so an untrusted generation containing no over-cap line replays at full speed with zero extra work, and a trusted generation containing an over-cap non-tombstone line (a legitimately huge memory record) skips it unparsed — trust remains purely a read-side skip optimization, never a mutation trigger (R42 F1). This makes any startup-ordering precondition unnecessary: there is no one-time legacy-migration step and no launch-ordering dependency between the backup manager and `start_periodic_tasks`, because the pre-upgrade file is simply the first untrusted generation and the first cycle that meets an over-cap line in it parses and applies through the same gate. Every mandatory work unit between two deadline checks remains bounded by the largest of the chunk size, the tombstone line cap, and the foreign-line ceiling. Each cycle completes at least one chunk of scan work or one full line before honoring the deadline, so pass position strictly advances every cycle — durably at line granularity via the traversal sidecar, in-memory at chunk granularity — and a pass over a snapshot of B bytes in L lines containing T tombstones terminates within ceil(T / cap) application-bound cycles plus scan-bound cycles bounded by B divided by the bytes scanned per deadline window — every cycle returning within the deadline plus at most one bounded work unit, with no record position starvable by repeated capped, interrupted, or restarted scans. Every tombstone arm — applied, LWW loss, retry, or indeterminate-cutoff — schedules ZERO mark-due invocations and ZERO listener fanouts, matching the live tombstone path. The committed change signal is owed the same way (R30 F1): the live storage path notifies registered change listeners only after its transaction commits and only for created/reactivated/newer-LWW outcomes (`src/gobby/storage/memories_crud.py:264-275`), and the rendered primary preserves that contract explicitly — the orchestrator invokes the storage change-listener fanout (`src/gobby/storage/memories_base.py`'s `_notify_listeners`, exposed as a public `notify_changed()` wrapper so the orchestrator never reaches a private method across the module boundary) exactly once after each resolved material non-tombstone outcome (`created`, `reactivated`, `updated`), zero times for `unchanged`/deduped outcomes, guaranteed pre-COMMIT rollbacks, and lost-response observations of a hidden or absent row (R47 F2 — no retry exists whose outcome could decide otherwise), and conservatively once when an indeterminate commit cannot be disproved — an exact-match verification or an ACTIVE mismatched row — the same conservative direction as the mark-due policy: a spurious notification costs one redundant listener pass, a missed one silently loses the committed change signal. Notification always runs after commit/outcome resolution and before the import result or the drained cancellation returns; tombstone primaries notify zero times, matching the live tombstone path. Draining the committed primary is not the whole obligation (R27 F1): a committed non-tombstone primary whose typed outcome is `created` or `reactivated` also owes 2.2's write-time mark-due wakeup, and that wakeup has NO durable intent — the recurring repair driver reconciles projections and `vector_needs_reindex` stamps, but nothing can recreate a missed `last_dreamed_at` clear — so re-raising cancellation before the hook is scheduled would silently lose the wakeup forever. The orchestrator therefore treats the owed hook as part of the same shielded drain: when the drained primary reports `created` or `reactivated`, it runs that record's outcome-aware mark-due invocation — identical scope mapping, outcome-specific anchor, semaphore bound, and failure isolation as the uncancelled path — as an owned task under `asyncio.shield` and drains it to its bounded terminal result before propagating cancellation, and only then stops before the next record; committed tombstone, `updated`, `unchanged`, and `deduped` drains owe nothing, exactly as in the uncancelled path. The obligation attaches at primary commit, not at the instant cancellation is caught (R28 F1): from the moment a record's primary commits a `created` or `reactivated` outcome until that record's outcome-aware mark-due invocation reaches its bounded terminal result, the record sits in an outcome-known post-commit scope, and cancellation observed ANYWHERE in that scope — while the shielded primary drain is completing, during the record's fenced secondary reconciliation await, in the gap after reconciliation and before hook ownership is established, or while an already-scheduled hook is in flight — drains exactly one outcome-aware mark-due invocation as an owned task under `asyncio.shield` to its bounded terminal result before propagating: an in-flight owned hook is drained rather than restarted, a not-yet-scheduled hook is started and drained, and the invocation never runs twice for one record. The interrupted fenced secondary reconciliation itself is NOT drained — its durable intent, stamped in the winning upsert arms, lets the repair driver finish the projections later — and the drained mark-due invocation tolerates the row's still-pending projections exactly as the primary-drain path does: keyword evidence admits from the committed content while the vector channel degrades best-effort. Only after the drained invocation terminates does cancellation propagate and the orchestrator stop before the next record. **Hard-delete cleanup is recreate-safe (R21 F4, extended R22 F2)**: four owners hard-delete memory rows or clean secondaries for rows that no longer exist — ordinary delete (`MemoryLifecycleService` purges after storage's physical `DELETE`), the dream hard purge (`purge_dream_hidden` hard-deletes rows then reconciles their artifacts), `reconcile_stores`' rowless-orphan reaping, and the public JSONL sync-import tombstone path (R22 F2): `MemoryBackupManager._import_memories_from_lines` (`src/gobby/sync/memories.py`) commits each winning memory tombstone via `apply_tombstone`'s raw `DELETE FROM memories` (`src/gobby/sync/tombstones.py`) in its own transaction and today reports the import result with NO vector, graph, or crossref cleanup at all — a successful public hard delete leaves the deleted memory recallable and consuming result windows until an unrelated reaper pass. The tombstone's LWW decision and per-tombstone transaction are preserved exactly; the import boundary collects every COMMITTED memory-tombstone id (a tombstone losing LWW or finding no row commits nothing and collects nothing) and, after each commit, routes it through the same recreate-safe cleanup below before reporting the import result, running it to completion on the bounded async surface from the synchronous import entry points; an import interrupted before its cleanup finishes leaves artifacts that the recurring reconciler's rowless-orphan reaping already owns (the durable backstop). Memory ids are deterministic uuid5(content, scope), so a same-content recreate can legitimately receive the SAME id concurrently with any of the four owners; a late artifact delete would then destroy the fresh row's vector or graph state. Each hard-delete cleanup therefore deletes the owned stale artifacts and then RE-CHECKS storage for an active same-id row: when one exists, it stamps that row's durable `vector_needs_reindex` intent (never clearing anything) and leaves the rebuild to the full fenced reconciliation via the repair driver — the recreated memory converges to fresh artifacts with no further mutation, and the delete-then-recheck order means a recreate committing at ANY point relative to the artifact delete is either untouched or caught by the recheck. The supersession purge keeps its exists-and-hidden visibility lock unchanged. **The intent flag has a single clearing owner (R18 F2)**: today two vector-only surfaces clear it wholesale after nothing but vector work — `IndexingService.reindex_embeddings` calls `mark_vectors_reindexed` for every project memory after its batch upsert, and `_run_global_embedding_reindex` does the same after its rebuild (both in `src/gobby/memory/services/indexing.py`) — and both are publicly reachable through the MCP, HTTP, and CLI reindex surfaces, so a reindex interleaving after a post-graph-removal reconciliation failure would erase the sole surviving graph/pending/crossref repair intent. Both paths therefore DROP their direct `mark_vectors_reindexed` calls: after their bulk vector work they route each STILL-FLAGGED row through the same full fenced reconciliation (the `reconcile_stores` repair machinery above), and `mark_vectors_reindexed` retains exactly ONE caller — the fenced reconciliation's terminal content-CAS clear — so the flag can only ever clear together with converged graph membership, pending marker, and crossrefs (unflagged rows carry no intent and need no clear; their vector refresh is unaffected). `get_pending_graph_memories` additionally filters `AND deleted_at IS NULL` before `ORDER BY`/`LIMIT` (defense in depth), and the KG worker (`_process_pending_graph_memories`, `src/gobby/sessions/lifecycle.py`) closes its selection-to-write window on EVERY path that may have written (R16 F4): `KnowledgeGraphService.add_to_graph` sets `made_progress` as soon as any entity, vector, relationship, or link lands and can return `partial_failure` or a made-progress connection-failure result AFTER writing, while today's worker calls `mark_graph_processed` only for success/noop and records failure otherwise — so the hidden-row check must not hang off the success path alone. The worker runs the post-add visibility decision BEFORE persisting any processed/failure/retry state: `mark_graph_processed` becomes conditional on `deleted_at IS NULL`, and on ANY possibly-written path — success, partial failure, a connection failure with `made_progress`, or an exception raised mid-add — a row found hidden gets the worker's just-written graph artifacts removed under the same fence, skipping the removal if the row is active again, since a restore's rebuild owns active-state artifacts. **Task cancellation is one of those possibly-written paths (R19 F3)**: `asyncio.CancelledError` is a `BaseException`, so the worker's `except Exception` around `add_to_graph` never sees it, and `SessionLifecycleManager.stop` cancels the KG queue task with a short drain before proceeding to teardown — a cancellation landing after `add_to_graph`'s first durable write (it sets `made_progress` at each of six write points before later awaits) would today unwind past the visibility decision entirely, and the new pending-row visibility filter guarantees the hidden row is never re-selected to clean those artifacts up. The worker therefore wraps the possibly-written span so cancellation runs the SAME post-add visibility check and fenced undo to completion under `asyncio.shield`, bounded by the fence's own `MUTATOR_RECONCILIATION_BUDGET_SECONDS` budget, then re-raises the cancellation; `SessionLifecycleManager.stop`'s drain contract awaits the cancelled worker for that same bound BEFORE any storage or graph teardown, so the shielded cleanup always has a live event loop and open clients to finish on. Restore-to-active still rebuilds recall state exactly as 2.1.12 specifies.

**Atomicity and semantics (E3+F5)**: one connection-aware storage transaction (`with self.db.transaction() as conn:` — pass `conn` through; `mark_dreamed`'s current self-owned transaction cannot participate, so add a connection-accepting variant in `src/gobby/storage/memories_dreams.py`). **Deterministic lock set including the result row (R6 F4)**: the resulting new-or-deduped memory is a lock participant too — its tag merge is a mutation, and its id is only discoverable via the in-transaction content-dedup lookup, so locking only sorted target rows admits a deadlock between two calls with inverted dedup/supersedes roles (A dedupes to X and supersedes Y while B dedupes to Y and supersedes X). Protocol: BEFORE any row read or mutation, acquire `pg_advisory_xact_lock` keys in ascending numeric order for the deduplicated supersedes ids PLUS the deterministic content-scope key derived from the normalized content and project scope (the same inputs as storage's uuid5 id seed) — the content key serializes concurrent inserts/dedupes of identical content even when the result row does not exist yet. Resolve the result id under those locks; if its key is not already held, lock it directly when it sorts after every held key, otherwise ROLL BACK (transaction-scoped locks release) and retry once with the full, now-known key set presorted, re-verifying the dedup resolution under the reacquired locks (bounded retries, then error). **Advisory keys serialize supersession calls — row locks fence ordinary mutators (R13 F4)**: update, rescope, soft-hide, and restore take no advisory locks, so between a plain classification read and the soft-hide UPDATE an ordinary mutator could commit a change that invalidates the classification while the call still commits. Once the full advisory key set is held, the transaction locks the resolved result row (when it already exists — the dedup case) and every existing target row with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` in ascending-id order BEFORE validation; classification reads those locked rows, and any ordinary mutator's write on them blocks until the supersession commits or rolls back, so the classification cannot be invalidated mid-flight (single-row mutators and 2.2's single-row fenced primitive cannot deadlock with the ordered multi-row acquisition). Only after the full set is held: deduplicate input ids; classify each target against the locked rows — **provenance-aware validation (R2 F3)**: (a) active, same scope → to-hide; (b) already soft-hidden AND the resulting new-or-deduped memory already carries the matching `supersedes:<id>` tag → already-satisfied no-op; (c) anything else (missing, cross-scope, self-reference via content-dedup, or hidden for any other reason or by any other memory) → invalid, reject the whole call before any mutation. Then perform the create/dedup tag merge and all pending soft-hides (`hidden_as="delete"`, recoverable; purged after `purge_delete_after_days`) on that same connection, tagging the new memory `supersedes:<old-id>`. Validation happens inside the transaction against locked rows, closing the concurrent-invalidation race. Idempotent retry (provenance-aware): a full-success retry — every target already hidden with matching provenance on the deduped memory — succeeds as a no-op; a partial retry completes exactly the missing soft-hides, treats provenance-satisfied targets as no-ops, and still rejects targets hidden without matching provenance; tag merges never duplicate provenance tags or drop caller tags. No partial writes.

**Typed write outcome (R3 F2, R4 F3)**: the write-outcome contract is a generic defined in new dependency-neutral `src/gobby/memory/write_result.py` — frozen dataclass `MemoryWriteResult[PayloadT]` with `memory: PayloadT` and `outcome: Literal["created", "reactivated", "deduped", "updated", "unchanged"]` (the last two produced only by the sync-import boundary's LWW upsert, R24 F1), zero gobby imports (verified safe: `gobby.memory` is a namespace package with no `__init__.py` side effects and nothing under `gobby.storage` imports `gobby.memory` today, so storage may import the module without cycles). The storage and protocol layers intentionally carry different payload types, so each layer names its specialization explicitly. **Storage keeps its surface (R5 F3)**: `MemoryCrudMixin.create_memory -> Memory` is a widely-consumed synchronous API with many direct callers and tests, so it is NOT re-typed; the outcome-producing surface is a new `MemoryCrudMixin.create_memory_with_outcome -> MemoryWriteResult[Memory]` holding the single implementation (dedup/reactivation/create classification), with `create_memory` delegating to it and unwrapping. `StorageAdapter.create` calls `create_memory_with_outcome`, converts the payload via `_to_record` and preserves the outcome, returning `MemoryWriteResult[MemoryRecord]`; `MemoryBackendProtocol.create` and the null backend (`src/gobby/memory/backends/null.py`) declare/return that `MemoryRecord` specialization, and the existing backend/protocol conformance tests (`tests/memory/test_backends.py`, `tests/memory/test_protocol.py`) update their exact return-shape assertions; `MemoryLifecycleService` consumes the protocol result's outcome and unwraps the record before the public response. **Lifecycle precheck removed (R5 F3)**: `MemoryLifecycleService.create_memory` currently returns the existing memory from a `content_exists` precheck (`src/gobby/memory/services/lifecycle.py:190`) BEFORE `backend.create` — an idempotent retry with `supersedes` would bypass the atomic supersession transaction and never produce the typed `deduped` outcome. Delete the precheck: every create traverses the storage chokepoint (whose deterministic-uuid5 collision path already returns the existing or reactivated row), and lifecycle branches on the returned outcome — `deduped` skips the embed/crossref/background-dedup/graph side effects (preserving today's duplicate behavior) while supersedes processing has already run atomically in storage. Public MCP/HTTP create responses are unchanged; 2.2's mark-due gate consumes the outcome at the lifecycle boundary.

**Acceptance:**

- 2.1.1 - `create_memory` with `supersedes` soft-hides each listed active memory and tags the new memory with `supersedes:<id>` provenance. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_soft_hides_and_tags`.
- 2.1.2 - Invalid supersedes ids (missing, cross-scope, or hidden without matching `supersedes:<id>` provenance on the resulting memory) reject the call before any mutation. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_validation_rejects`.
- 2.1.3 - MCP tool schema documents the parameter. file: `src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/memory.py`.
- 2.1.4 - An injected mid-operation failure (after create, before soft-hides complete) rolls back the entire operation — no new memory row, no tags, no hidden memories. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_rollback_on_failure`.
- 2.1.5 - Idempotent retry: a full-success retry (all targets already hidden with matching provenance) succeeds as a no-op; a partial retry completes exactly the missing soft-hides, preserves caller tags, and produces no duplicate provenance tags. The full-success retry is exercised through the public lifecycle/HTTP chain, proving the storage dedup path — not a lifecycle precheck — produces the outcome (R5 F3). test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_idempotent_retry`. test: `tests/servers/routes/test_memory_routes.py::test_create_memory_supersedes_retry`.
- 2.1.6 - Mixed target sets: one provenance-satisfied hidden target (no-op) plus one active target (hidden) succeed together; adding one target hidden without matching provenance rejects the whole call before any mutation. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_mixed_targets`.
- 2.1.7 - Every named surface threads the parameter end-to-end: HTTP route request with `supersedes` exercises the full chain; StorageAdapter/protocol conformance covers the parameter (including the null backend implementing the protocol signature). test: `tests/servers/routes/test_memory_routes.py::test_create_memory_supersedes`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_backend_protocol_supersedes`.
- 2.1.8 - The typed write outcome distinguishes created, reactivated, and deduped results with each layer's exact specialization: `create_memory_with_outcome` returns `MemoryWriteResult[Memory]` while `create_memory` keeps returning `Memory` for existing storage callers, StorageAdapter/protocol/null backend return `MemoryWriteResult[MemoryRecord]` with the outcome preserved across `_to_record` conversion, lifecycle has no `content_exists` early return and branches on the outcome (deduped skips secondary side effects), backend/protocol conformance tests assert the new return shapes, and public responses are unchanged. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_write_outcome_types`. test: `tests/memory/test_backends.py`. test: `tests/memory/test_protocol.py`.
- 2.1.9 - Concurrent inverse-role regression (R6 F4): call A deduping to memory X while superseding Y runs concurrently with call B deduping to Y while superseding X — no PostgreSQL deadlock, both calls complete with one fully atomic outcome each, and no caller or provenance tag is lost on either row; a concurrent-dedup pair targeting the SAME result row serializes its tag merges (both callers' tags survive). test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_inverse_role_concurrency`.
- 2.1.10 - MCP guard interaction (R8 F4): non-empty `supersedes` bypasses the ephemeral-note guard and the speculative-task redirect and runs the full create+supersession chain; the same content without `supersedes` still trips each guard (skip and redirect respectively); ordinary non-guarded MCP creates forward `supersedes` end-to-end. test: `tests/mcp_proxy/tools/test_memory.py::test_create_memory_supersedes_guard_bypass`.
- 2.1.11 - Post-commit secondary-index reconciliation (R8 F5, R10 F2, R13 F5): a successful supersession removes each superseded id's vector and graph artifacts via the visibility-locked purge while crossrefs are retained; an injected secondary-store failure leaves the committed create and soft-hides intact (logged, success still returned); an idempotent retry re-purges provenance-satisfied ids (crash repair); a purge finding the row already hard-deleted skips secondary deletion entirely (R14 F3); and pending-graph selection never returns hidden rows — `deleted_at IS NULL` filters before `ORDER BY`/`LIMIT` (R15 F3). Visibility-aware store reconciliation (R19 F1): with one superseded-and-purged hidden row, one active row whose vector was deleted out-of-band, and a leftover graph node for a second hidden row (an induced partial purge), the REAL recurring `reconcile_stores` pass repairs the active row's vector through the fenced reconciliation, removes the hidden row's leftover graph node as an orphan via the visibility-locked purge step, and leaves BOTH hidden rows absent from vector and graph recall — no missing-backfill ever re-upserts a hidden id. Crossref starvation regression (R10 F2, revised R13 F5): with a hidden superseded row holding higher-similarity crossrefs than an active related memory and a `get_related` limit the stale links would exhaust, `get_related` returns the active lower-ranked memory — hidden rows' links are filtered in SQL before `ORDER BY`/`LIMIT`, not merely dropped after hydration (asserted against a fetch window the stale links would have exhausted). test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_purges_secondary_indices`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_crossref_starvation_removed`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_reconcile_stores_hidden_row_stays_absent`.
- 2.1.12 - Restore-after-supersede (R13 F5): through the public MCP/HTTP restore path, a superseded-then-restored memory is active AND recallable — vector search finds it (re-embedded), the graph holds it, and `get_related` serves its retained crossrefs. Both purge/restore interleavings hold: purge holding the row lock first → the blocked restore completes and its rebuild repairs the deletes; restore committing first → the late purge observes the active row and skips — rebuilt state is never purged. Concurrent-recreate interleaving (R14 F3): with the superseded row hard-deleted and a same-content create recommitting the SAME deterministic id with fresh vector/graph artifacts, a late purge for that id skips (missing-then-recreated row) and the recreated active memory remains vector- and graph-recallable. Hidden-content-update restore (R17 F3): a memory superseded with content A, updated to content B while hidden (primary applies; the fenced reconciliation skips wholesale), then restored — A's retained crossrefs are deleted under the reactivating transaction and never observable on the active row, `get_related` serves links rebuilt from B, and a control row restored WITHOUT an intervening content change keeps its retained crossrefs unchanged with zero rebuild. Fenced restore rebuild (R18 F1): a restore whose post-commit rebuild is DELAYED while a newer content update, a rescope, and a re-supersession-plus-purge (each exercised separately) commit first skips wholesale — every secondary store reflects the newer owner in every schedule; and a content-clean restore whose rebuild is induced to fail before its terminal CAS clear leaves the reactivation-stamped intent set and converges — vector, pending-graph marker, and retained crossrefs all live on the active row — via the repair driver with NO further user mutation. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_restore_after_supersede_rebuilds`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_restore_hidden_content_update_rebuilds_crossrefs`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_restore_reconciliation_stale_skips`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_restore_clean_rebuild_failure_converges`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_purge_restore_interleavings`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_purge_missing_row_recreate_safe`.
- 2.1.13 - Row-lock fencing against ordinary mutators (R13 F4; outcomes split per mutator, R14 F5): with a supersession paused after acquiring its advisory keys and row locks, a concurrent content/tag update, rescope, soft-hide, and restore (each exercised separately) blocks until the supersession commits — never mutating a row whose classification-relevant state changed mid-call. Post-commit outcomes follow each mutator's own semantics: update and soft-hide leave the target hidden with the mutator's PRIMARY effect applied afterward (the fenced secondary writes observe the hidden row and skip, R15 F3), rescope leaves it hidden in its new scope (its payload and pending-graph writes likewise skipped), and a blocked restore commits afterward, ends ACTIVE, and completes the R13 F5 secondary-index rebuild (the 2.1.12 contract). Conversely a mutator committing BEFORE lock acquisition is observed by validation — a restored-to-active target classifies as an ordinary active to-hide, while a rescoped-away target or one hidden without matching provenance rejects the whole call atomically. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_row_lock_fencing`.
- 2.1.14 - Bounded cardinality (R13 F7): through MCP and HTTP, a list exceeding `MAX_SUPERSEDES_IDS` unique ids and a malformed (non-UUID) id are each rejected before any advisory lock, row lock, or mutation (asserted via unchanged rows and zero acquired locks); a list whose duplicates collapse under the cap succeeds with each id counted once. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_cardinality_bounds`. test: `tests/servers/routes/test_memory_routes.py::test_create_memory_supersedes_bounds`.
- 2.1.15 - Listener boundary (R13 F8): a registered memory change listener fires exactly once after a successful supersession commit for each of the created, deduped-with-hides, and partial-retry outcomes; it never fires for a full provenance-satisfied no-op retry nor on an injected rollback; and no notification is observable before the commit (asserted by listener-observation ordering against the transaction). test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_listener_boundary`.
- 2.1.16 - Absolute cleanup budget (R14 F4, R15 F2): with a REAL blocked or trickling Falkor response (each per-read wait under `socket_timeout` while total elapsed exceeds the budget) inside a per-id purge step, the cleanup is aborted at `SUPERSESSION_CLEANUP_BUDGET_SECONDS` by `asyncio.timeout` cancellation, the async visibility transaction is hard-closed and the PostgreSQL row lock releases within the budget (a concurrently blocked restore then completes and rebuilds recall state), the fault is logged, and a later retry re-purges the id; the same bound is proven for a trickling Qdrant delete. Blocked row-lock acquisition (R15 F2): with the target row's lock held by a foreign transaction outlasting the budget, the cleanup's `FOR UPDATE` acquisition — on its dedicated async connection — is aborted at the budget by 0.3's terminal sequence (cancellation, hard local close, second cancellation — R16 F1), the event loop stays responsive throughout (a sentinel task completes promptly during the block), no lock waiter, connection, worker, or thread survives, and a later retry re-purges the id. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_cleanup_budget_releases_row_lock`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_cleanup_blocked_lock_bounded`.
- 2.1.17 - Hidden-row reindex fence (R15 F3, R16 F3, R16 F4): for content update and rescope separately, BOTH purge orderings hold — fenced reconciliation first → the purge, blocked on the fence, removes the artifacts afterward; purge first → the fenced reconciliation observes the hidden row and skips wholesale (no vector upsert, no payload write, no pending-graph mark, no graph removal, no crossref delete or rebuild) — and in both orderings the hidden row is absent from vector and graph recall afterward, while restore-to-active still rebuilds it (the 2.1.12 contract). Restore interleaving (R16 F3): a content update whose secondary reconciliation is delayed while the row is superseded, purged, and then restored — the restore, serialized against the fence, never lands between the reconciliation's constructive writes and its destructive deletes: in every schedule the restored active memory retains its graph presence and crossrefs. Pending-graph selection filter: a hidden row queued as pending is never returned by `get_pending_graph_memories`. KG-worker window (R16 F4): a row hidden after selection while `add_to_graph` is in flight is never marked processed and the worker's fenced undo removes its just-written artifacts — proven for the success-status path AND for a partial-write-then-failure interleaving (`add_to_graph` writes entities then returns `partial_failure`, and separately a made-progress connection failure; the worker persists its failure/retry state only AFTER the fenced undo has removed the artifacts); the same row restored before the undo keeps its rebuilt artifacts (no undo on an active row); and for TASK CANCELLATION (R19 F3): a real `SessionLifecycleManager.stop()` issued while `add_to_graph` is paused after its first durable graph write, with the row hidden mid-flight — the shielded post-add visibility check and fenced undo complete within the drain bound, the worker re-raises the cancellation and terminates, and no graph artifact, background task, or dangling connection survives teardown. Crossref fence containment (R19 F4): a fenced content reconciliation induced to fail AFTER `rebuild_for_memory` has inserted a crossref on the held fence connection but BEFORE the terminal CAS commits — the fence rollback leaves NO crossref row observable (nothing escaped the transaction), the primary-committed intent survives, and the repair driver's retry rebuilds the links to convergence. Stale-reconciliation ordering (R17 F2, extended cross-kind R19 F2): for concurrent content updates, concurrent rescopes, AND both cross-kind orderings — a delayed rescope reconciliation entering after a newer content update's commit, and a delayed content reconciliation entering after a newer rescope's commit — mutation A's DELAYED fenced reconciliation observes the (content, scope) snapshot mismatch and skips wholesale — PostgreSQL and every secondary store (Qdrant vector and payload, graph, crossrefs) reflect B in every schedule, and in the delayed-rescope-after-content-update ordering specifically, the intent flag stays SET after the rescope reconciliation skips (a skipped reconciliation never reaches its CAS, and even a reached CAS executes against the rescope's scheduling content, which no longer matches — defense in depth) until the content update's own reconciliation — or, with that induced to fail, the repair driver — converges every secondary store and only then clears it. Durable failure convergence (R17 F4): a fenced content reconciliation blocked AFTER the external graph removal applies but before its SQL commits is terminated at `MUTATOR_RECONCILIATION_BUDGET_SECONDS` by 0.3's terminal sequence with the row lock released within the budget; the primary-committed `vector_needs_reindex` intent survives the rollback, and the repair driver (`reconcile_stores`) re-runs the full fenced reconciliation — graph membership, pending marker, and crossrefs converge with NO further user mutation. Single-owner intent clearing (R18 F2): after an induced post-graph-removal reconciliation failure, a PUBLIC project embedding reindex and a global embedding reindex (each exercised separately) interleave before repair with the fenced repair itself induced to fail during the reindex — neither clears the row's intent while graph membership, the pending marker, or crossrefs remain unconverged (the reindex routes the flagged row through the full fenced reconciliation, never a wholesale clear) — and recurring `reconcile_stores` then converges every secondary store and only then clears the flag. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_hidden_row_secondary_write_fence`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_vector_reindex_preserves_reconciliation_intent`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_stale_mutator_reconciliation_skips`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_reconciliation_failure_durable_repair`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_restore_interleaved_content_refresh`. test: `tests/storage/test_storage_memories.py::test_pending_graph_filters_hidden`. test: `tests/sessions/test_lifecycle.py::test_kg_worker_hidden_row_undo`. test: `tests/sessions/test_lifecycle.py::test_kg_worker_partial_failure_hidden_undo`. test: `tests/sessions/test_lifecycle.py::test_kg_worker_cancellation_hidden_undo`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_cross_kind_stale_reconciliation_skips`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_crossref_rebuild_fence_rollback`.
- 2.1.18 - Rescope replaces scope-dependent projections (R20 F1): for a project→global promotion and a project-A→project-B move (each exercised separately), the fenced rescope reconciliation leaves the graph node carrying ONLY the new scope — the old project id is absent even though the node pre-existed (remove-then-re-extract, never an in-place coalesce) — and crossref state reflects only the new scope: old-scope links are deleted on the fence connection, rebuilt links come from the new scope's candidates, and a `get_related` fetch window the old-scope links would have exhausted serves only new-scope counterparts. The 2.1.17 orderings extend to this shape: a delayed rescope reconciliation entering after a newer content update skips wholesale touching neither graph nor crossrefs, and an induced failure between the graph-node removal and the crossref rebuild leaves the intent set until the repair driver converges node scope, pending marker, and crossrefs with no further mutation. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_rescope_replaces_scope_projections`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_rescope_reconciliation_failure_converges`.
- 2.1.19 - Bulk rebuilds are fenced and every activation sweeps (R20 F2, completed R21 F2 and R22 F1): with a hide, a content update, and a rescope (each exercised separately) landing between a bulk rebuild's row snapshot and its per-row write, the MCP `rebuild_crossrefs` surface, the HTTP rebuild-crossrefs route, `IndexingService.rebuild_indices`, the PROJECT embedding reindex, the no-swap GLOBAL embedding reindex, and the standalone KG rebuild each skip that row wholesale — no crossref, vector, or graph artifact from the stale snapshot is observable afterward (the hidden row stays absent from every store; the updated or rescoped row reflects only its own mutator's reconciliation) — and the crossref surfaces prove the fence sits in the shared `rebuild_crossrefs_for_memory` seam (the same fence behavior observed through MCP and HTTP alike). Post-activation sweep (R22 F1): for BOTH the swap-requiring global embedding reindex and the public embedding switch (each exercised separately), a create, a hide, a content update, and a rescope paused-before-activation then resumed land safely — after the swap or alias flip, the sweep stamps intent for the created row (absent from the embedded snapshot) and the updated and rescoped rows (divergent (content, scope)), never clearing anything, and the repair driver converges each against the new collection; the hidden row's vector is removed via the visibility-locked purge and stays absent; and the embedded snapshots carry full scheduling identity — a rebuilt vector's payload preserves `project_id` (scope-filtered search still finds it). Rowless sweep branch (R23 F1): for BOTH activation surfaces (each exercised separately), a hard delete paused-after-snapshot and landing before activation leaves the deleted id absent from recall after the swap or alias flip — the sweep classifies the embedded id as rowless and routes it through the recreate-safe cleanup against the newly active collection — and a pause-cleanup/recreate-same-id case proves the deterministic same-id recreate converges to fresh artifacts via the stamped intent while a truly deleted row stays absent from recall. Startup routing (R22 F1): the daemon's empty-Qdrant startup rebuild schedules the IndexingService global reindex — no id/content-only snapshot and no bespoke `rebuild_vector_store` path survive, and a row created during the startup rebuild is never deleted by a stale-id pass and ends recallable. For the standalone KG rebuild specifically, a partial-write failure and a cancellation mid-`add_to_graph` each run the fenced undo to completion before any processed/failure state persists, per the R19 F3 contract. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_bulk_rebuilds_respect_fence`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_kg_rebuild_stale_snapshot_undo`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_reindex_paths_respect_fence`. test: `tests/memory/test_vectorstore_init.py::test_startup_rebuild_routes_through_reindex`. test: `tests/ai/test_embedding_switch_runner.py::test_switch_activation_sweep_stamps_divergent_rows`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_activation_sweep_purges_rowless_snapshot_ids`. test: `tests/ai/test_embedding_switch_runner.py::test_switch_activation_purges_rowless_snapshot_ids`.
- 2.1.20 - Intent discovery is Qdrant-independent and repair is truthfully partial (R20 F3, R21 F3): with a row left graphless and crossref-less by an induced post-graph-removal reconciliation failure, a `reconcile_stores` pass with NO vector store configured and one whose `scroll_ids` raises (each exercised separately) still consumes the durable flag and converges graph membership and the pending marker, while the flag stays SET and the crossrefs remain absent — crossref candidate discovery is a vector similarity search, so crossref and vector work both stay owed; after Qdrant recovers, the next pass completes the vector upsert AND the crossref rebuild and only then clears the flag via the terminal CAS. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_intent_repair_without_qdrant`.
- 2.1.21 - Null-project repair uses the rescope primitive (R20 F4): a hidden row with a NULL project is never selected (discovery filters `deleted_at IS NULL`); a row hidden between discovery and apply is skipped with NO committed scope change and no vector, graph, or crossref artifact recreated (selection-then-supersede race); and a successful repair stamps intent and commits the scope under the row lock, with secondaries converging only through the fenced rescope reconciliation — an induced reconciliation failure leaves the flag set and the repair driver converges with no further mutation. test: `tests/memory/test_manager.py::test_null_project_repair_fenced`.
- 2.1.22 - Create, reactivation, and dedup write through the fence (R21 F1): with the primary create committed and its post-commit reconciliation paused, a supersession that hides and purges the row commits, then the reconciliation resumes — it observes the hidden row and skips wholesale, no vector, crossref, or graph artifact reappears, and the stamped intent converges to absence via the repair driver; the same regression holds for a REACTIVATED soft-hidden duplicate and for the background dedup's richer-content update (now flowing through the ordinary content-update seam — a paused dedup reconciliation resuming after a newer content update skips wholesale and the newer content's vector survives); a create with `supersedes` purges every superseded target BEFORE the result memory's crossref build runs (the build's similarity search never links against a purged target's vector); and an induced failure between the create commit and its reconciliation leaves the intent set, with the repair driver converging the new row's vector, crossrefs, and pending-graph mark with no further mutation. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_create_reconciliation_fenced`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_dedup_update_fenced`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_supersedes_purge_precedes_crossref_build`.
- 2.1.23 - Hard-delete cleanup is recreate-safe (R21 F4, extended R22 F2): ordinary delete and the dream hard purge each remove the deleted row's vector and graph artifacts even though the row is already physically gone when cleanup runs (the missing-row skip belongs ONLY to the supersession purge); and for each of the four hard-delete owners — ordinary delete, dream GC, `reconcile_stores`' rowless-orphan reaping, and the sync-import tombstone path — a same-content recreate committing the SAME deterministic id concurrently with the cleanup (paused before its artifact delete, resumed after the recreate) ends with the recreated row active and fully recallable: the post-delete recheck finds the active row, stamps its intent, and the repair driver rebuilds vector and graph state — proven for both Qdrant and graph artifacts. Sync-import tombstones (R22 F2): a public JSONL import applying a winning memory tombstone removes the row AND its vector, graph, and crossref artifacts before the import result is reported (the memory is no longer recallable through any store); a tombstone losing LWW to a newer local row or targeting a missing row commits nothing and triggers no cleanup; and the pause-recreate-resume regression above holds through the real public import entry point for both Qdrant and graph artifacts. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_hard_delete_cleanup_removes_artifacts`. test: `tests/memory/test_create_supersedes.py::test_hard_delete_recreate_race_converges`. test: `tests/sync/test_memory_sync.py::test_import_tombstone_cleans_secondary_indices`. test: `tests/sync/test_memory_sync.py::test_import_tombstone_recreate_race_converges`.
